Library: Nucleolus and ribosomes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Key findings

- The nucleolus is the site of ribosomal RNA (rRNA) transcription, pre-RNA processing, and ribosome subunit assembly
- Cells with high protein synthesis, such as cancer cells and liver cells, tend to have more nucleoli

## Ribosome sizes

| Cell type | Whole ribosome | Small subunit | Large subunit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Prokaryotic | 70S | 30S | 50S |
| Eukaryotic | 80S | 40S | 60S |

## Why it matters

Several antibiotics act on the prokaryotic subunits. Aminoglycosides and tetracyclines bind the 30S subunit, while macrolides, clindamycin, and linezolid bind the 50S subunit.

> Prokaryotic ribosomes are 70S (30S + 50S); eukaryotic ribosomes are 80S (40S + 60S).

Library: Lysosomes vs. peroxisomes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Key findings

| Feature | Lysosomes | Peroxisomes |
|---|---|---|
| Origin | Golgi body | Not from the Golgi body |
| Where enzymes are made | Rough ER, then passed to the Golgi | Free ribosomes (like mitochondria) |

- Lysosomes are highly acidic
- Peroxisomes carry out beta oxidation of long-chain fatty acids and alpha oxidation of branched-chain fatty acids
- Peroxisomal catalase breaks down hydrogen peroxide into water

## Related disorders

| Disorder | Defect | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| I-cell disease | NAG-1 phosphotransferase (Golgi) | Enzymes aren't recognized by the M6P receptor, so lysosomes are empty |
| Zellweger syndrome | Peroxisomes | Very-long-chain and branched-chain fatty acids accumulate |
| Refsum disease | Alpha oxidation | Phytanic acid can't be metabolized |
| Adrenoleukodystrophy | Peroxisomal acyl-CoA synthetase | Very-long-chain fatty acids build up; X-linked recessive |

> Lysosomal enzymes are made on the rough ER and sent through the Golgi; peroxisomal enzymes are made on free ribosomes.

Library: Centrioles and basal bodies
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Key findings

- In non-dividing cells, centrioles attach to the inner side of the plasma membrane to form a basal body
- A basal body can form the base of a flagellum or a beating (motile) cilium
- In all other cells, the basal body forms a nonmotile primary cilium

> In most cells, the basal body forms a nonmotile primary cilium.

Library: Membrane phospholipid asymmetry
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Key findings

| Leaflet | Lipids |
|---|---|
| Inner (negative) | PS, PE, PI |
| Outer | PC, SM, GL |

- Flippases move lipids in, and floppases move them out
- The endoplasmic reticulum membrane is symmetric because of scramblase activity
- An influx of calcium can make scramblases display PS on the outer leaflet, which signals apoptosis

> PS on the outer leaflet is a signal for apoptosis.

Library: Proteasomes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Key findings

- The regulatory particle (RP, regulatory proteins) binds ubiquitin
- The core particle (CP, core proteins) does the degrading
- Red blood cells have proteasomes and no other organelles

> Red blood cells have proteasomes but no other organelles.

Library: Heteroplasmy and the microsomal fraction
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cell Structure and Intracellular Organelles

## Heteroplasmy

The severity of a mitochondrial mutation changes from one child to another in the same family.

## Microsomal fraction

Vesicles derived from the smooth and rough ER, the Golgi body, and the plasma membrane that form during centrifugation.

> Heteroplasmy explains why siblings with the same mitochondrial mutation can be affected to different degrees.

Library: Amino acid derivatives
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Amino Acids, Proteins, and Enzymes

## Key findings

| Amino acid | Derivatives |
|---|---|
| Tryptophan | Serotonin, niacin, melatonin |
| Tyrosine | Dopamine, norepinephrine, epinephrine, thyroxine, melanin |
| Histidine | Histamine |
| Beta-alanine | Coenzyme A |

## Clinical link

Hartnup disease is a defect in neutral amino acid transport. Losing tryptophan leads to niacin deficiency.

> Tryptophan is the precursor of niacin, so Hartnup disease causes niacin deficiency.

Library: Essential, ketogenic, and glucogenic amino acids
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Amino Acids, Proteins, and Enzymes

## Essential amino acids (PVT TIM HaLL)

Phenylalanine, valine, tryptophan, threonine, isoleucine, methionine, histidine, leucine, and lysine.

## Ketogenic vs. glucogenic

| Group | Amino acids | Memory hook |
|---|---|---|
| Ketogenic | Leucine, lysine | The two L's |
| Both glucogenic and ketogenic | Isoleucine, phenylalanine, tryptophan, threonine | TTIP |

## Protein sequences

Peptide sequences are written from the N terminus to the C terminus.

> Leucine and lysine are ketogenic; isoleucine, phenylalanine, tryptophan, and threonine are both glucogenic and ketogenic.

Library: Disulfiram, fomepizole, and ALDH2 deficiency
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Amino Acids, Proteins, and Enzymes

## Pathway

Alcohol → (alcohol dehydrogenase) → acetaldehyde → (aldehyde dehydrogenase) → acetic acid

## Key findings

| Drug or condition | Target | Type | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| Disulfiram | Aldehyde dehydrogenase | Irreversible inhibitor | Acetaldehyde builds up and worsens hangover symptoms; used to treat alcoholism |
| Fomepizole | Alcohol dehydrogenase | Competitive inhibitor | Stops toxic downstream products in methanol and ethylene glycol poisoning |
| ALDH2 deficiency | Mutant aldehyde dehydrogenase | Enzyme mutation | Km rises and Vmax falls; acetaldehyde buildup causes "Asian flush" |

## Related drug

Metronidazole also inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase and causes a disulfiram-like reaction, so it can't be given to patients taking disulfiram.

> Disulfiram blocks aldehyde dehydrogenase, so acetaldehyde accumulates.

Library: Creatine kinase isoenzymes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Amino Acids, Proteins, and Enzymes

## Key findings

| Isoenzyme | Main tissue |
|---|---|
| CK-1 | Brain (CNS diseases) |
| CK-2 | Myocardium (heart) |
| CK-3 | Skeletal muscle and myocardium |

> CK-2 is the myocardial isoenzyme.

Library: Formins and Arp2/3: linear vs. branched actin
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Actin

## Key findings

| Nucleator | Filament | Structures |
|---|---|---|
| Formins | Linear | Microvilli, filopodia |
| Arp2/3 complex | Branched at a 70-degree angle | Lamellipodia |

## Formin structures

- Microvilli are stable, uniform structures. Bundling proteins cross-link the parallel filaments, a stable cap at the plus end stops growth, and the minus end attaches to the terminal web of the cortical actin cytoskeleton
- Filopodia grow and retract rapidly because their cap is less stable. Their filaments are cross-linked by fascin

## Arp2/3 structures

- Lamellipodia can contract using myosin. Actomyosin contraction creates shape changes, as in epithelial wound healing and folding
- ActA is a bacterial scaffold protein that induces rapid Arp2/3-based assembly

## Pathogens

Some bacterial and fungal pathogens hijack the cortical actin cytoskeleton, using a comet tail of rapid assembly behind them for propulsion. Listeria moves with actin rockets.

> Formins make linear filaments; the Arp2/3 complex starts branches at a 70-degree angle.

Library: Actin toxins: phalloidin, jasplakinolide, and cytochalasin D
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Actin

## Key findings

| Toxin | Source | Effect on actin |
|---|---|---|
| Phalloidin | Death cap mushrooms | Binds three monomers and blocks both assembly and disassembly: hyperstabilizes subunits and blocks ADP release |
| Jasplakinolide | Sea sponges | Blocks disassembly, so filaments only grow ("actin goes on a journey") |
| Cytochalasin D | Fungi | Caps the plus end, so filaments shorten until they're gone |

## Death cap mushroom poisoning

- The toxicity comes from alpha-amanitin, which inhibits RNA polymerase II and stops mRNA synthesis, causing massive hepatocyte death
- The liver is the main target; listed symptoms include extreme hunger, jaundice, and seizures or coma

> Death cap toxicity comes from alpha-amanitin, which inhibits RNA polymerase II.

Library: Red blood cell membrane cytoskeleton
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Actin

## Key findings

- Spectrin is a flexible coiled dimer
- F-actin and ankyrin anchor the network to the membrane
- Red cells with a mutated spectrin cytoskeleton often have problems with shape and flexibility

## Disorders

| Disorder | Protein | Key finding |
|---|---|---|
| Hereditary spherocytosis | Ankyrin (spectrin and band 3 also listed) | Spherocytes without central pallor; Coombs negative |
| Hereditary elliptocytosis | Spectrin | Elongated red cells |

> Hereditary spherocytosis is linked to ankyrin; hereditary elliptocytosis is linked to spectrin.

Library: Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophy
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Actin

## Mechanism

Muscular dystrophy comes from mutations in the protein complex that secures contracting muscle cells to the extracellular matrix. Dystrophin helps attach actin fibers to the ECM by anchoring to the cortical actin cytoskeleton.

## Duchenne muscular dystrophy

- Severe
- Mutations in the actin-complex binding domains of dystrophin
- Frameshift or nonsense mutations leave dystrophin truncated or absent
- X-linked recessive
- Progressive muscle weakness and calf pseudohypertrophy

## Becker muscular dystrophy

- Mutations in the spacer domains of dystrophin
- These don't directly disrupt protein-protein interactions

> Duchenne mutations hit dystrophin's actin-binding domains; Becker mutations hit the spacer domains.

Library: Microtubule structure and dynamic instability
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cytoskeleton and Microtubules

## Key findings

- The basic unit is a dimer of alpha and beta tubulin. Beta is at the plus end and alpha is at the minus end
- Each microtubule has 13 protofilaments
- Gamma tubulin sits on the MTOC and helps start microtubule formation
- Microtubule-associated proteins such as tau can modify tubulin tails

## Dynamic instability

- GTP-bound tubulin: growth (rescue)
- GTP hydrolysis: shrinkage (catastrophe)
- The GTP cap is on the plus (beta) end

## Drug link

Paclitaxel stabilizes microtubules so spindle fibers can't pull apart, arresting cells in M phase. Docetaxel, vincristine, and vinblastine are also grouped as M-phase microtubule inhibitors.

> GTP-bound tubulin grows (rescue); GTP hydrolysis leads to shrinkage (catastrophe).

Library: Tau and Alzheimer disease
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cytoskeleton and Microtubules

## Key findings

- Tau is a microtubule-associated protein
- Neurofibrillary tangles (NFTs) are aggregates of tau
- Hyperphosphorylated tau no longer binds microtubules and forms aggregates
- It's still an open question whether the disease comes from losing tau's normal function or from the toxicity of the aggregates

> Neurofibrillary tangles in Alzheimer disease are aggregates of hyperphosphorylated tau.

Library: Kinesin and dynein motors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cytoskeleton and Microtubules

## Kinesin

- Two polypeptide heads
- Needs cargo to be activated, which keeps it from wasting ATP

## Dynein

- Two types: cytoplasmic and axonemal
- Much larger than kinesin
- Can't function by itself; it needs activators, cargo, and cargo adaptors
- Activators: dynactin and Lis1

## Motor disorders

| Disorder | Defect | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| Perry syndrome | Dynactin 1 (DCTN1) | Parkinson-type symptoms that don't respond to medication; tremor, weight loss, depression; autosomal dominant |
| Spinal muscular atrophy and ALS | Bicaudal-D, a dynein cargo adaptor | Dynein becomes hyperactive |
| Lissencephaly | Lis1 (Dcx in the X-linked form) | Neurons fail to migrate to the outer cortex; smooth brain |

> Bicaudal-D mutations make dynein hyperactive.

Library: Motile vs. nonmotile cilia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cytoskeleton and Microtubules

## Key findings

| Feature | Motile cilia | Nonmotile (primary) cilia |
|---|---|---|
| Arrangement | 9+2 | 9+0 |
| Central pair | Present | Absent |
| Dynein | Axonemal dynein links microtubules and drives a power stroke | Absent |
| Location | Lung epithelium, female reproductive tract, sperm flagellum | A basal body with the cilium sticking out like an antenna on the apical surface |

- Cilia are built on microtubules, while microvilli are built on actin

> Motile cilia are 9+2 with axonemal dynein; primary cilia are 9+0 with no central pair and no dynein.

Library: Ciliopathies
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cytoskeleton and Microtubules

## Key findings

| Disease | Cilia type | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Primary ciliary dyskinesia | Motile | Mucus builds up in the lungs, throat, and sinuses, leading to infections; infertility in males and females |
| Kartagener syndrome | Motile | A worse form of primary ciliary dyskinesia: infertility, sinusitis, situs inversus |
| Polycystic kidney disease | Nonmotile | The primary cilium can't detect fluid flow, disrupting calcium signaling; tubule cells proliferate and form cysts |
| Bardet-Biedl syndrome | Nonmotile | Pleiotropic: vision loss, polydactyly, kidney cysts, weight gain, characteristic facial features |

> Situs inversus with sinusitis and infertility points to Kartagener syndrome.

Library: SGLT1 and SGLT2
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Membrane Transport

## Key findings

| Feature | SGLT1 | SGLT2 |
|---|---|---|
| Location | Intestine | Kidney |
| Substrates | D-glucose and D-galactose | Glucose only |
| Affinity | Higher than SGLT2 | Lower than SGLT1 |

- Active transport happens on the apical side (external milieu); passive transport happens on the basolateral side (internal milieu)
- Sodium is pumped out on the basolateral side, and the symporter brings sugar in with sodium as sodium moves down its concentration gradient

## Clinical links

- Glucose-galactose malabsorption: an SGLT1 defect leaves children unable to absorb glucose and galactose, causing osmotic diarrhea
- SGLT2 inhibitors (phlorizin analogs such as Jardiance, Farxiga, and Invokana) treat type 2 diabetes. Glucose isn't reabsorbed into the bloodstream, and the extra water lost with it can cause dehydration. They also raise the chance of yeast infections

> SGLT2 inhibitors make the kidney spill glucose, which brings dehydration and yeast infections as side effects.

Library: GLUT transporters
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Membrane Transport

## Key findings

| Transporter | Tissues | Key role |
|---|---|---|
| GLUT1 | Ubiquitous | Average affinity; basal glucose uptake; critical for glucose transport across the blood-brain barrier |
| GLUT2 | Liver, pancreatic beta cells, kidney, small intestine | Low affinity, so it only works after eating; glucose sensor for insulin secretion |
| GLUT4 | Muscle, heart, adipose | Responds to insulin and exercise; not on the cell surface until insulin activates it |
| GLUT5 | Intestine, kidney, testis, sperm | Fructose |

- Because exercise also brings GLUT4 to the surface, people with type 2 diabetes are encouraged to work out

> GLUT4 responds to both insulin and exercise.

Library: GLUT transporter disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Membrane Transport

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| GLUT1 deficiency syndrome | GLUT1 | Seizures, developmental delay, hypotonia, low CSF glucose (hypoglycorrhachia), because the blood-brain barrier depends on GLUT1 |
| GLUT2 inactivation | GLUT2 | Glucose can't enter beta cells, so insulin isn't released: hypoinsulinemia and diabetes |
| Fructose malabsorption | GLUT5 | Fructose stays in the gut lumen and can cause diarrhea |
| MODY | Glucokinase | A rare form of diabetes linked with features of both type 1 and type 2 |

## FDG imaging

18FDG is a substrate of all GLUTs and enters every cell in the body. It has to be injected because the GI tract absorbs glucose through SGLTs. GLUT1 is overexpressed in cancer cells.

> GLUT1 deficiency presents with seizures and developmental delay because the brain depends on GLUT1.

Library: FPHHI and sulfonylureas
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Membrane Transport

## Mechanism

In pancreatic beta cells, restricting potassium exit keeps the membrane more positive. Calcium channels then open, and insulin is released.

## Key findings

- Familial persistent hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia of infancy (FPHHI): mutations restrict K+ exit from beta cells, so they release too much insulin. Autosomal recessive
- Mutations that increase K+ release have the opposite effect and lower insulin levels
- Sulfonylureas work by essentially the same mechanism as FPHHI. They block K+ exit so calcium channels open and insulin is released, which is how they treat type 2 diabetes

> Sulfonylureas mimic FPHHI: blocking K+ exit from beta cells triggers insulin release.

Library: Amino acid transport disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Membrane Transport

## Key findings

Amino acids are moved by secondary active transporters, and most individual amino acids are carried by more than one transporter.

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Hartnup disease | Intestinal and renal transport of neutral amino acids | More neutral amino acids lost in urine; tryptophan loss causes niacin deficiency; cerebellar ataxia; autosomal recessive |
| Cystinuria | Luminal absorption of cationic amino acids and cystine (COLA: cystine, ornithine, lysine, arginine) | Positive cyanide-nitroprusside test; yellow, waxy kidney stones; urine smells like rotten eggs; autosomal recessive |
| Cystinosis | Lysosomal transporter that moves cystine out of the lysosome | Cystine accumulates in lysosomes of nearly every organ; eventually leads to Fanconi syndrome |

> Cystinuria is a gut and kidney transport defect; cystinosis is a lysosomal transport defect. They're easy to confuse.

Library: Drug targets, occupancy, and KD
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacodynamics

## Drug targets

- Small-molecule drugs act on GPCRs, enzymes, ligand- and voltage-gated ion channels, transcription factors, nuclear receptors, transport and structural proteins, and RNA
- GPCRs account for about 12% of targets and ion channels about 19%
- Biologics include monoclonal antibodies, growth factors, and allergens

## Occupancy

- Drugs work by occupying their target, and binding is reversible
- KD is the concentration of drug needed to bind half of the targets

> KD is the drug concentration that occupies half of the targets.

Library: Agonists, antagonists, and inverse agonists
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacodynamics

## Two-state model

GPCRs and ion channels exist in inactive and active forms, and drugs shift the equilibrium one way or the other.

| Ligand | Effect |
|---|---|
| Full agonist | Shifts toward the active conformation |
| Partial agonist | Shifts partially toward the active conformation |
| Competitive antagonist | Occupies the site and competes with the agonist for binding; higher KD |
| Inverse agonist | Shifts toward the inactive conformation |

## Look-alikes

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Potency | The concentration of drug needed to bind the target (KD) |
| Efficacy | The maximal physiological effect |

> An inverse agonist shifts the receptor toward the inactive state; a competitive antagonist only occupies the site.

Library: Biased and allosteric ligands
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacodynamics

## Ligand bias

- Ligand bias means favoring one state over another, a more complex model than the two-state model
- Many receptors have multiple signal transduction pathways, and some ligands favor one pathway over another. Those ligands are "biased" toward that pathway

## Allosteric ligands

- Allosteric ligands bind a site other than the one occupied by the natural ligand (the orthosteric site)
- They can be positive or negative allosteric modulators
- They fine-tune a response by modulating affinity, efficacy, and agonism

> Allosteric ligands bind away from the orthosteric site and fine-tune the response.

Library: Therapeutic index
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacodynamics

## Key findings

- Therapeutic index = TD50 / ED50 (toxic dose / effective dose)
- A larger number is better, because the effective and toxic doses are farther apart and less likely to overlap
- Vancomycin is an example of a drug with a narrow therapeutic index that requires drug monitoring

> A larger therapeutic index means a wider gap between the effective and toxic doses.

Library: G protein alpha subunits
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cellular Signaling

## Key findings

| Subunit | Effector | Pathway |
|---|---|---|
| Gαs | Stimulates adenylyl cyclase | AC/cAMP/PKA |
| Gαi | Inhibits adenylyl cyclase | AC/cAMP/PKA |
| Gαq | Phospholipase C (PLC) | PLC/IP3/calcium |
| Gαt | PDE6 (cyclic GMP) | Rhodopsin |
| Gα12/13 | RhoA | RhoA/ROCK |

## Rhodopsin

In the eye, 11-cis-retinal is the molecule that reacts to light and causes the conformational change in rhodopsin.

## Clinical link

Bordetella pertussis toxin ADP-ribosylates the Gαi protein, which raises cAMP.

> Gαs raises cAMP, Gαi lowers it, and Gαq works through PLC, IP3, and calcium.

Library: cAMP and IP3/calcium pathways
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cellular Signaling

## AC/cAMP/PKA pathway

Gαs → adenylyl cyclase → cyclic AMP (second messenger) → PKA

## PLC/IP3/calcium pathway

1. Gαq activates phospholipase C (PLC)
2. PLC splits PIP2 into DAG and IP3
3. IP3 activates calcium channels
4. Calcium activates calmodulins

## Clinical link

Bacillus anthracis and Bordetella pertussis share a mechanism: both raise cAMP levels, which causes edema.

> Phospholipase C splits PIP2 into DAG and IP3.

Library: Smooth muscle contraction signaling
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cellular Signaling

## Contraction

- Gα12/13 → RhoA-GTP → ROCK → inhibits myosin light chain phosphatase (MLCP), which normally allows relaxation
- Gαq → IP3 → calcium release → activates myosin light chain kinase (MLCK)

## Relaxation

- Nitric oxide → cGMP → PKG1, which activates MLC phosphatase
- PKG2 inhibits calcium release

> Contraction comes from turning on MLCK (Gαq, calcium) or turning off MLCP (Gα12/13, RhoA, ROCK).

Library: Nitric oxide, cGMP, and PDE-5
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cellular Signaling

## Pathway

Nitric oxide (NO) → soluble guanylate cyclase (sGC) → cGMP → PKG1 and PKG2

## Key findings

- PKG1 activates MLC phosphatase, causing relaxation
- PKG2 inhibits calcium release, also causing relaxation
- PDE-5 converts cGMP into GMP, ending the signal. Drugs are made to block PDE-5

> PDE-5 breaks down cGMP, so blocking it prolongs NO-driven relaxation.

Library: Growth factor signaling: RAS-MAPK, PI3K-AKT, and Wnt
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Cellular Signaling

## RAS-MAPK pathway

Receptor tyrosine kinase is phosphorylated → Grb2 (scaffolding protein) → SOS (guanine nucleotide exchange protein) → RAS → RAF → MEK → ERK → transcription factors

## PI3K-AKT pathway

- The survival pathway
- PI3K converts PIP2 to PIP3 (second messenger), which binds and activates AKT
- Drives survival, protein synthesis, and metabolism
- PTEN is the phosphatase that opposes PI3K

## Wnt/beta-catenin pathway

Beta-catenin is normally destroyed by the destruction complex. When Wnt binds, beta-catenin is no longer degraded.

## Clinical link

HER2 amplification signals along the MEK and AKT pathways and increases cell proliferation. It's amplified in about 30% of breast cancers.

> PTEN is the phosphatase that opposes PI3K in the survival pathway.

Library: Drug absorption and metabolism
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacokinetics

## Absorption

- Absorption is getting a drug into circulation
- Permeation through the lipid bilayer depends on size, hydrophobicity, and the lipid-water coefficient
- P-glycoprotein (P-GP) pumps foreign substances, including drugs, out of cells

## Metabolism

- Cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes carry out phase I reactions in drug metabolism
- They usually add a hydroxyl group, making the drug more hydrophilic so the kidneys can excrete it

## Clinical links

- Rifampin is a CYP inducer, and fluconazole inhibits CYP450
- Barbiturates, alcohol, and sulfonamides induce P450, which can precipitate attacks of acute intermittent porphyria

> CYP450 phase I reactions usually add a hydroxyl group so the drug can be excreted by the kidneys.

Library: Bioavailability, volume of distribution, and clearance
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacokinetics

## Key findings

| Parameter | Meaning | Formula |
|---|---|---|
| Bioavailability (F) | How much of the drug reaches systemic circulation | F = AUC oral / AUC IV |
| Volume of distribution (Vd) | The apparent volume in which a quantity of drug is distributed | Vd (mL) = amount (mg) / concentration (mg/mL) |
| Clearance (Cl) | Volume of plasma cleared of a drug per unit of time | Not given as a formula |

> Bioavailability compares the oral AUC to the IV AUC.

Library: Loading and maintenance doses
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Pharmacokinetics

## Key findings

- Loading dose = Vd × target concentration
- Maintenance dose = Cl × target concentration, or (Cl × target concentration) / F when bioavailability is factored in

> The loading dose depends on volume of distribution; the maintenance dose depends on clearance.

Library: Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 2: Cellular, Molecular, and Genetic Foundations / Patterns of Inheritance

## Key findings

- p is the frequency of allele A, and q is the frequency of allele a
- p² is the frequency of AA
- 2pq is the frequency of Aa
- q² is the frequency of aa

> 2pq is the frequency of heterozygotes (Aa).

Library: ATP generation and creatine phosphate
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cellular Energetics

## Key findings

- About 90% of ATP is made from ADP by ATP synthase (complex V)
- Substrate-level phosphorylation occurs during glycolysis
- Transphosphorylation lets higher-energy phosphates such as creatine phosphate make ATP
- Phosphocreatine is a ready source of energy for ATP synthesis in muscle, and creatine kinase catalyzes the reaction

## Amphibolic pathways

A process that involves both anabolism and catabolism is amphibolic. The citric acid cycle is the example: it handles the aerobic catabolism of carbohydrates, lipids, and amino acids, and it also supplies intermediates as starting points for biosynthesis.

> About 90% of ATP comes from ATP synthase (complex V); glycolysis makes ATP by substrate-level phosphorylation.

Library: Adenylate kinase and energy charge
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cellular Energetics

## Key findings

- Adenylate kinase catalyzes the interconversion of AMP, ADP, and ATP, and it acts as a metabolic monitor
- The adenylate (energy) charge measures the energy stored in a cell
- Energy charge = ([ATP] + ½[ADP]) / ([ATP] + [ADP] + [AMP])
- A cell aims for an energy charge of about 0.9

## What the charge controls

| Energy charge | Response |
|---|---|
| High | Catabolic pathways shut down |
| Low | Anabolic pathways shut down and catabolic pathways turn on to make ATP |

> Cells hold their energy charge near 0.9; a falling charge turns on catabolism.

Library: Caloric value and respiratory quotient
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cellular Energetics

## Caloric value

Caloric value is the energy released when 1 gram of a molecule is completely oxidized.

| Fuel | kcal/g | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Carbohydrate | 4 | More oxidized; needs less oxygen to release its energy |
| Fatty acid | 9 | More reduced; needs more oxygen |

## Respiratory quotient

- Respiratory quotient (RQ) = CO2 produced / O2 consumed
- It lets you estimate the mix of fuels being oxidized

> RQ = CO2 produced / O2 consumed.

Library: Citric acid cycle regulation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cellular Energetics

## Key findings

| Enzyme | Inhibited by | Stimulated by |
|---|---|---|
| Citrate synthase | ATP, NADH, succinyl-CoA, citrate | Not listed |
| Isocitrate dehydrogenase | ATP, NADH | ADP, NAD+ |
| Alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase | ATP, NADH, succinyl-CoA | Not listed |

- The inhibitors are downstream products, and the stimulators are precursors of ATP production

## Anabolic precursors

- The citric acid cycle provides intermediates for biosynthesis
- The mitochondrial tricarboxylate transporter exchanges citrate from the mitochondrial matrix for malate. Citrate lyase then turns citrate into acetyl-CoA in the cytoplasm for fatty acid synthesis
- Inhibitors of this transporter reduce fatty acid synthesis

> Isocitrate dehydrogenase is inhibited by ATP and NADH and stimulated by ADP and NAD+.

Library: Glycolysis regulation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Hexokinase is the first step, converting glucose to glucose-6-phosphate, which traps glucose in the cell
- The two regulated irreversible steps are both highly allosterically controlled

| Step | Enzyme | Activated by | Inhibited by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fructose-6-P → fructose-1,6-bisphosphate | Phosphofructokinase (PFK) | AMP, fructose-2,6-bisphosphate | ATP, citrate |
| Phosphoenolpyruvate → pyruvate | Pyruvate kinase | Fructose-1,6-bisphosphate | ATP, acetyl-CoA |

> PFK is activated by AMP and fructose-2,6-bisphosphate and inhibited by ATP and citrate.

Library: Anaerobic glycolysis and lactic acidosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Red blood cells have no mitochondria, so they rely on glycolysis for ATP
- Anaerobic glycolysis also occurs in hypoxic conditions
- Lactate dehydrogenase converts pyruvate to lactate, which the liver can then use

## Causes of lactic acidosis

- Hypoxia, as in COPD, exercise, or anemia
- Reduced clearance of upstream products such as pyruvate. In pyruvate dehydrogenase deficiency, pyruvate can't become acetyl-CoA
- Reduced clearance from renal or hepatic dysfunction

> Red cells have no mitochondria, so glycolysis is their only source of ATP.

Library: 2,3-BPG and hemoglobin oxygen affinity
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Key findings

- 2,3-bisphosphoglycerate (2,3-BPG, or DPG) is made from 1,3-bisphosphoglycerate
- It stabilizes the low-affinity state of hemoglobin

| Condition | DPG | Curve | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| High ATP | More DPG (1,3-BPG is pushed toward the DPG pathway) | Right shift | Oxygen binds less well and is delivered to tissues more easily |
| High ADP | Less DPG | Left shift | Oxygen binds well but is harder to deliver |

## Fetal hemoglobin

Fetal hemoglobin doesn't bind DPG, so it has a higher oxygen affinity and takes up oxygen from the mother.

> More DPG shifts the curve right and unloads oxygen to tissues.

Library: Glycolytic enzyme deficiencies in red cells
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Mechanism

When glycolysis runs too slowly, red cells can't make ATP fast enough. The sodium/potassium pumps stop working, and the cells become echinocytes (thorny projections), leading to hemolytic anemia.

## Key findings

| Deficiency | DPG | Curve | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hexokinase | No DPG | Left shift | Most fatal |
| Phosphofructokinase | No DPG | Left shift | Not listed |
| Pyruvate kinase | DPG present | Right shift | Most common; chronic, autosomal recessive, worldwide distribution |

- Listed symptoms of hemolytic anemia from defective glycolysis include fainting, low blood pressure, and shortness of breath

> Deficiencies before the DPG branch point (hexokinase, PFK) remove DPG and shift the curve left; pyruvate kinase deficiency shifts it right.

Library: Glycogen synthesis and breakdown
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Portal circulation sends all nutrient-rich blood through the liver before the rest of the body
- Glycogenin is the primer for making glycogen from glucose
- Glucose-6-phosphatase turns glucose-6-phosphate into free glucose

| Enzyme | Role | Partner enzyme | Allosteric control |
|---|---|---|---|
| Glycogen synthase | Makes glycogen | Branching enzyme | Activated by glucose-6-phosphate |
| Glycogen phosphorylase | Breaks down glycogen | Debranching enzyme | Inhibited by glucose and glucose-6-phosphate |

## Hormonal and muscle control

- Glucagon and epinephrine activate glycogen phosphorylase
- In skeletal muscle, which needs ATP quickly, glycogen phosphorylase is activated by high AMP and calcium. Glycolysis is also activated by AMP

> Glucagon and epinephrine activate glycogen phosphorylase; in muscle, AMP and calcium do too.

Library: Gluconeogenesis and the Cori cycle
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

## Gluconeogenesis

Gluconeogenesis runs glycolysis backward, converting many non-carbohydrates into glucose. It can make glucose without carbohydrates in the diet, and the liver uses it to convert amino acids to glucose.

## Cori cycle

1. Pyruvate from skeletal muscle is turned into lactate and alanine
2. These travel to the liver
3. Gluconeogenesis in the liver turns them into glucose
4. The glucose goes back to the muscle

> In the Cori cycle, muscle lactate and alanine become glucose in the liver and return to muscle.

Library: Glycogen storage diseases
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Significance of Metabolic Pathways Involved in Glucose Homeostasis

Most glycogen storage diseases come from decreased glycogen breakdown. Liver forms cause inter-meal hypoglycemia and an enlarged liver; muscle forms cause exercise intolerance and muscle cramping or wasting. Memory hook for types I to VII: "Viagra Pills Cause A Major Hard-on."

| Type | Deficient enzyme | Tissue | Key findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| I, von Gierke | Glucose-6-phosphatase | Liver and kidney | Most lethal; hepatomegaly, inter-meal hypoglycemia, failure to thrive; gluconeogenesis can't compensate because its glucose can't be dephosphorylated either |
| II, Pompe | Lysosomal alpha-1,4-glucosidase | All organs | Glycogen builds up in lysosomes; severe muscle damage; cardiorespiratory failure, often death before age 2 |
| III, Cori | Debranching enzyme | Liver and muscle | Mild hypoglycemia; can improve as the liver adjusts |
| IV, Andersen | Branching enzyme | Liver | Unbranched glycogen is attacked by the immune system; liver cirrhosis; lethal |
| V, McArdle | Muscle glycogen phosphorylase | Muscle | Exercise intolerance, cramps, rhabdomyolysis, myoglobinuria; "second wind" after about 10 minutes |
| VI, Hers | Liver glycogen phosphorylase | Liver | Like a milder von Gierke; gluconeogenesis keeps blood glucose up |
| VII, Tauri (Tarui) | Muscle phosphofructokinase | Muscle | Glucose is shunted into glycogen; rhabdomyolysis, myoglobinemia |

> Von Gierke is the most severe because glucose-6-phosphatase is needed for both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.

Library: Total daily energy expenditure
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Energy Balance and Over and Under Nutrition

## Fuel energy

| Fuel | kcal per gram | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Fat | 9 | Fatty acids produce energy; sterols don't |
| Carbohydrate | 4 | |
| Protein | 4 | Inefficient, because amino acids must be deaminated to enter the carbon cycle |

## Components of daily expenditure

| Component | Share |
|---|---|
| Basal metabolic rate (BMR) | About 50% |
| Exercise-associated thermogenesis (EAT) | About 25% |
| Non-exercise activity thermogenesis (NEAT) | About 15% |
| Thermic effect of food (TEF) | The rise in metabolic rate from eating |

- The thermic effect of food is greatest for protein and smallest for lipid, because protein takes more energy to break down

> Basal metabolic rate makes up about half of total daily energy expenditure.

Library: Tissue-specific fuel use
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Energy Balance and Over and Under Nutrition

## Key findings

| Tissue | Fuel pattern |
|---|---|
| Brain | Glucose, because other metabolites can't cross the blood-brain barrier |
| Muscle | Stores 75% of the body's sugar for its own use only |
| Liver | Stores sugar for all organs; converts amino acids to glucose by gluconeogenesis |
| Adipose | Stores 95% of the body's fat; low glucose triggers fat release |
| Heart | Stores almost no fuel but uses a lot; mainly burns fat and prefers ketones over glucose to save glucose for the brain |

> Muscle glycogen is for muscle only; liver glycogen supplies the rest of the body.

Library: Body mass index
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Energy Balance and Over and Under Nutrition

## Key findings

- BMI = weight (kg) / height (m)²

| BMI | Category |
|---|---|
| Below 18.5 | Thin |
| 18.5-24.9 | Proportional |
| 25-29.9 | Grade I overweight |
| 30-39.9 | Grade II overweight |
| Above 40 | Grade III overweight |

> BMI = weight in kilograms divided by height in meters squared.

Library: Leptin and genetic obesity
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Energy Balance and Over and Under Nutrition

## Leptin

- Adipose releases leptin in response to insulin, cortisol, and the products of glucose metabolism
- High leptin lowers the desire to eat until fat stores fall, which then lowers leptin release (a feedback loop)
- Genetic loss of leptin can cause human obesity, and it can be treated with leptin infusion
- Most people with obesity are hyperleptinemic, which points to leptin resistance

## Genes associated with obesity

- Pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC)
- Melanocortin 4 receptor (MC4R)
- Leptin

> Most people with obesity have high leptin levels, which reflects leptin resistance.

Library: Fasting, deprivation, and starvation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Energy Balance and Over and Under Nutrition

## Three levels of energy deprivation

| Stage | What happens |
|---|---|
| Prolonged fasting | Glucose is spared for the brain; glycogen is broken down, then glucose is made from amino acids |
| Extended deprivation | Carbohydrate sources are gone; ketone production from fat rises; non-essential plasma proteins drive gluconeogenesis |
| Starvation | Fat reserves are depleted and protein is the only source left; essential cellular proteins are degraded |

> In starvation, loss of immune function leads to infection, the most common cause of death.

Library: Galactosemia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Background

Infants get galactose from milk from birth, and it's mostly processed in the liver.

## Key findings

| Type | Deficient enzyme | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Classic (type 1) | Galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT) | Most fatal; presents in the first weeks after birth with vomiting, diarrhea, hepatomegaly, jaundice, cataracts, failure to thrive, and susceptibility to E. coli infections; autosomal recessive |
| Type 2 (non-classical) | Galactokinase | Cataracts, because galactose becomes galactitol in the lens |
| Type 3 (non-classical) | GALE | Like type 1 but milder and very rare |

## Mechanism

Phosphate gets trapped in the intermediate, causing transient hypophosphatemia. Without inorganic phosphate, the electron transport chain and citric acid cycle shut down, fatty acids can't be used for energy, and glycolysis is left as the only energy source, so pyruvate is converted to lactate.

> Classic galactosemia presents in the first weeks of life, once milk feeding begins.

Library: Fructose metabolism disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Normal metabolism

- Fructose is metabolized in the liver, and its products enter glycolysis
- Fructokinase isn't regulated, so it bypasses hormonal control and feeds glycolysis in an unregulated way (the "fat gene")
- Fructokinase is much faster than aldolase B, so fructose-1-phosphate can accumulate and lower free phosphate

## Key findings

| Disorder | Deficient enzyme | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Essential fructosuria | Fructokinase | Benign; normal life |
| Hereditary fructose intolerance | Aldolase B | Fructose-1-phosphate accumulates, trapping phosphate; hypoglycemia, vomiting, jaundice, diarrhea, liver damage |

## Treatment

Hereditary fructose intolerance requires a fructose-, sucrose-, and sorbitol-free diet. Sorbitol from the polyol pathway can also make fructose, which can be lethal.

> Hereditary fructose intolerance looks like galactosemia; the difference is timing, since it appears when fructose-containing foods are introduced.

Library: Polyol pathway
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Key findings

- Aldose reductase converts aldose sugars into their sugar alcohols
- These alcohols are very hydrophilic and can't leave the cell
- Sorbitol accumulates in the lens and nerve cells, which have a lower capacity to process it, causing swelling and cataracts
- In galactosemia, the same pathway turns galactose into galactitol and causes cataracts

## Diabetic complications

With chronic hyperglycemia, glucose is pushed down the polyol pathway, and sorbitol accumulation contributes to diabetic cataracts, neuropathy, retinopathy, and nephropathy.

> Sorbitol trapped in the lens and nerves drives diabetic cataracts and neuropathy.

Library: Pentose phosphate pathway
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Where it's active

- Dividing tissues (nucleotide synthesis)
- Tissues under high oxidative stress, such as red blood cells
- Tissues with high biosynthetic activity that need electron donors

## Products

- NADPH keeps the cell in a reducing state and helps eliminate oxidative radicals
- Ribose-5-phosphate is used to make purines and pyrimidines

## Key enzymes

- Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) is the rate-limiting enzyme of the oxidative branch. It makes NADPH from NADP+; NADPH inhibits it and NADP+ promotes it. The gene is on the X chromosome
- Transketolase needs thiamine pyrophosphate and is cytosolic, unlike most thiamine-dependent enzymes. Red cell transketolase activity estimates the body's thiamine reserves

## Glutathione

- Glutathione is an antioxidant tripeptide. Reduced glutathione (GSH) reacts with hydrogen peroxide and becomes oxidized (GSSG)
- Glutathione reductase uses NADPH to regenerate GSH
- Glutathione peroxidase contains four selenium ions and breaks down hydrogen peroxide

> G6PD is the rate-limiting enzyme of the oxidative branch and the source of NADPH.

Library: G6PD deficiency
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Mechanism

Without G6PD, red cells can't make NADPH, so glutathione can't be reduced and the cells are exposed to oxidative stress. This causes lipid peroxidation and hemolysis. Oxidized hemoglobin precipitates as Heinz bodies, and the spleen removes them, leaving bite cells.

## Key findings

- The main symptom is hemolytic anemia
- It isn't chronic; it flares during infections or with oxidizing drugs
- Triggers: infection (most common), fava beans, sulfa drugs such as TMP-SMX, and primaquine
- Back pain and hemoglobinuria days after exposure
- X-linked recessive; about 1 in 10 African American males carry the mutation
- Partially protective against P. falciparum

## Smear

- Heinz bodies are clumps of irreversibly denatured hemoglobin attached to the membrane, seen with supravital staining
- Bite cells

## Management

Avoid triggers, give supportive care during a crisis, and check G6PD before giving primaquine or tafenoquine.

> Heinz bodies and bite cells after an infection or oxidizing drug point to G6PD deficiency.

Library: Respiratory burst and chronic granulomatous disease
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Minor Pathways of Hexose Metabolism and Associated Diseases

## Respiratory burst

Activated phagocytes increase oxygen consumption during infection to make hypochlorous acid (HOCl), which kills bacteria.

NADPH oxidase → superoxide dismutase → myeloperoxidase → HOCl

## Chronic granulomatous disease (CGD)

- Deficient NADPH oxidase; about 75% of mutations are X-linked and 25% autosomal
- Low superoxide, hydrogen peroxide, and HOCl, so catalase-positive organisms escape destruction
- Severe, recurrent infections and granulomas, such as S. aureus infections
- Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) test: low green signal means CGD
- Prophylaxis: TMP-SMX with itraconazole

## Marker of oxidative damage

Malondialdehyde is a three-carbon lipid breakdown product. Measuring it estimates lipid peroxidation and membrane damage.

> CGD leaves patients vulnerable to catalase-positive organisms.

Library: Fatty acid activation and the carnitine shuttle
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Triglyceride breakdown

- Fatty acid oxidation is stimulated by glucagon, epinephrine, and ACTH
- Adipose triglyceride lipase (ATGL) is turned on by glucagon and off by insulin, and it's active when phosphorylated
- Triglyceride → diglyceride → monoglyceride

## Activation and entry

- Acyl-CoA synthetase (ACS) adds CoA to the fatty acid for import into mitochondria, using 2 ATP

| Chain length | Route into mitochondria |
|---|---|
| Short and medium | Directly |
| Long | Carnitine shuttle (CAT-I and CAT-II) |
| Very long | Shortened in the peroxisome first |

- A deficiency of carnitine or of CAT-I/CAT-II causes muscle weakness and cramps with exercise

> Long-chain fatty acids need the carnitine shuttle; very-long-chain fatty acids go to the peroxisome first.

Library: Beta oxidation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Four steps

1. Oxidation
2. Hydration
3. Oxidation
4. Thiolysis

- The first oxidation uses acyl-CoA dehydrogenase, which has three mitochondrial forms for different chain lengths: SCAD, MCAD, and LCAD

## Alcohol and fatty liver

Ethanol metabolism uses up NAD+, and the third step of beta oxidation needs NAD+. With beta oxidation blocked, fat builds up in the liver, leading to fatty liver and cirrhosis.

> Ethanol consumes NAD+, blocking beta oxidation and causing fatty liver.

Library: MCAD deficiency
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Key findings

- Medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase no longer works
- Intolerance to fasting, impaired ketogenesis, hypoglycemia, vomiting, lethargy, coma, and death
- Omega oxidation is upregulated when beta oxidation is blocked and serves as a biological marker of MCAD deficiency

## Related conditions

- Sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS) is believed to be due in part to MCAD deficiency: glucose runs out during sleep and fatty acids can't be broken down
- Unripe ackee fruit causes acute MCAD deficiency by inhibiting acyl-CoA dehydrogenase, producing MCAD-like symptoms

> MCAD deficiency presents with fasting hypoglycemia and impaired ketogenesis.

Library: Odd-chain, unsaturated, alpha, and omega oxidation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Key findings

| Situation | What happens |
|---|---|
| Odd number of carbons | The last cut leaves three-carbon propionyl-CoA, which can later become glucose via a carboxylase and a mutase |
| Unsaturated fats | Isomerases correct the bond configuration so beta oxidation can continue |
| Long branched-chain fatty acids | Alpha oxidation in peroxisomes, catalyzed by alpha-hydroxylase at the alpha carbon |
| Beta oxidation blocked | Omega oxidation in the endoplasmic reticulum, from the opposite end of the fatty acid |

- Loss of the carboxylase or mutase that processes propionyl-CoA causes propionic or methylmalonic aciduria

> Odd-chain fatty acids end in propionyl-CoA, the one part of a fatty acid that can become glucose.

Library: Zellweger syndrome, adrenoleukodystrophy, and Refsum disease
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Zellweger syndrome | No functional peroxisomes | Very-long-chain and branched-chain fatty acids accumulate; jaundice, hepatomegaly, seizures, hypotonia, facial abnormalities; autosomal recessive; diet change |
| Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) | Peroxisomal acyl-CoA synthetase | Very-long-chain fatty acids build up in the nervous system, adrenals, and testes; adrenal insufficiency is often the first sign, then progressive demyelination; X-linked recessive |
| Refsum disease | Alpha oxidation (alpha-hydroxylase) | Can't metabolize phytanic acid; scaly skin, shortened toes, ataxia, anosmia, cataracts, night blindness; autosomal recessive |

## Treatment of Refsum disease

A diet without phytanic acid, and plasmapheresis.

> Refsum disease is an alpha oxidation defect, so dietary phytanic acid must be avoided.

Library: Ketone bodies
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Key findings

- Ketones are usually made during prolonged fasting
- They're made in liver mitochondria when the citric acid cycle is less active
- Acetyl-CoA units are joined to make acetoacetate, and beta-hydroxybutyrate is used for energy in peripheral tissues
- Thiophorase converts acetoacetate back to acetyl-CoA for the citric acid cycle in peripheral tissues, and it's absent from the liver
- The heart prefers ketones over glucose, which saves glucose for the brain

> The liver makes ketones but can't use them, because it lacks thiophorase.

Library: Fatty acid synthesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of Lipid Metabolism

## Steps

1. Acetyl-CoA leaves the mitochondria as citrate (exchanged for malate by the tricarboxylate transporter)
2. Citrate lyase turns citrate back into acetyl-CoA in the cytoplasm
3. Acetyl-CoA carboxylase (ACC) makes malonyl-CoA
4. Fatty acid synthase grows the chain by adding more malonyl-CoA

## Essential fatty acids

Linoleic and linolenic acids are essential fatty acids.

> Acetyl-CoA carboxylase makes malonyl-CoA, the building block fatty acid synthase adds to the chain.

Library: HMG-CoA reductase and statins
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cholesterol Metabolism

## Short-term regulation

- HMG-CoA reductase is the rate-limiting step of cholesterol synthesis, and it's active when not phosphorylated
- High insulin promotes phosphatase activity, activating the reductase
- High glucagon promotes kinase activity, inactivating it

## Long-term regulation

- The HMG-CoA reductase gene is regulated by cellular cholesterol
- High cholesterol keeps the regulatory complex in the endoplasmic reticulum
- Low cholesterol moves the complex to the Golgi, and a signal eventually reaches the nucleus to turn the gene on

## Drugs

Statins, such as lovastatin, inhibit HMG-CoA reductase.

> Insulin activates HMG-CoA reductase by dephosphorylating it; glucagon inactivates it.

Library: Bile acids and cholelithiasis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Cholesterol Metabolism

## Elimination

- Cholesterol leaves the body by conversion to bile acids
- 7-alpha-hydroxylase converts cholesterol to primary bile acids and salts; inhibiting it prevents cholesterol breakdown

## Cholelithiasis

- When cholesterol is greater than 15% of bile, it can aggregate into gallstones
- Causes include 7-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency and bile salt deficiency
- Listed approaches: ultrasound, cholecystectomy, sonic disruption, and bile salt administration

> 7-alpha-hydroxylase is the enzyme that turns cholesterol into bile acids.

Library: Lipoprotein structure and lipases
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Structure

- Free fatty acids travel on albumin; triglycerides, cholesterol, and cholesterol esters travel in lipoproteins
- A lipoprotein has a core of hydrophobic lipids (triglycerides and cholesterol esters) and a surface of cholesterol, phospholipids, and apolipoproteins
- Some apolipoproteins stay with their particle, and others move between particles
- Lipoproteins are classified by density; chylomicrons and VLDL are too large to leave blood vessels

## Lipases

| Lipase | Made by | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| Lipoprotein lipase (LPL) | Adipocytes, skeletal and cardiac myocytes | Anchored to endothelium; must be activated by ApoCII on chylomicrons and VLDL; breaks down lipoprotein triglycerides for storage or fuel |
| Hepatic lipase (HL) | Hepatocytes | Hydrolyzes lipids in HDL and acts on IDL |

> Lipoprotein lipase needs ApoCII to break down triglycerides in chylomicrons and VLDL.

Library: Chylomicron and VLDL assembly
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Key findings

| Feature | Chylomicrons | VLDL |
|---|---|---|
| Made in | Intestinal mucosal cells | Hepatocytes |
| Carries | Absorbed dietary lipids (mostly triglyceride) | Lipids synthesized in the liver or taken up from chylomicrons |
| Secreted into | Lymph | Interstitial fluid |
| Apolipoprotein | ApoB48 | ApoB100 |

- Lipids are assembled in the smooth ER, and ApoB proteins are made in the rough ER
- ApoB48 and ApoB100 come from the same gene; hepatocytes read the whole gene
- ApoB48 lacks the LDL receptor binding region, so it can't interact with the LDL receptor
- Microsomal triglyceride transfer protein (MTTP) moves triglycerides and cholesterol esters onto ApoB and is needed to assemble both particles
- Long and very-long-chain fatty acids go into chylomicrons; shorter fatty acids go straight to the liver through the portal system

> ApoB48 is on chylomicrons and ApoB100 is on VLDL and LDL; both come from one gene.

Library: Abetalipoproteinemia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Key findings

- Defective microsomal triglyceride transfer protein (MTTP), so chylomicrons and VLDL can't be made
- Lipid malabsorption with steatorrhea (fatty stool)
- Retinal degeneration, ataxic neuropathy, and acanthocytes (spiculated red cells)
- Many of these findings come from fat-soluble vitamin deficiency

## Treatment

The diet is usually shifted to shorter fatty acids, which don't need to be packaged into chylomicrons or VLDL.

> MTTP defects block chylomicron and VLDL assembly, causing steatorrhea and acanthocytes.

Library: LDL and the LDL receptor
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Where LDL comes from

- IDL that isn't taken up (from excess calories) trades triglyceride for cholesterol ester with HDL and hands its ApoE to HDL
- Hepatic lipase then acts on the ApoE-free IDL, raising its cholesterol ester content and forming LDL
- LDL carries ApoB100

## LDL receptors

- LDL delivers cholesterol esters to tissues through LDL receptors
- Receptors are made in the ER, processed in the Golgi, and sit in coated pits on the membrane
- They bind ApoB100, the complex is endocytosed, and lysosomes release the cholesterol
- Uptake depends on receptor number, which depends on intracellular cholesterol

## Atherosclerosis

The longer LDL stays in plasma, the more likely it is to be oxidized. Macrophage scavenger receptors take up oxidized LDL and aren't downregulated by cellular cholesterol, so macrophages become foam cells that contribute to plaques.

> Scavenger receptors aren't downregulated by cholesterol, so macrophages keep taking up oxidized LDL and become foam cells.

Library: HDL and reverse cholesterol transport
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Synthesis

- ApoA1 is the main structural protein of HDL, made in the liver and small intestine
- ABCA1 loads cholesterol and phospholipids from peripheral cells onto HDL
- LCAT moves a fatty acid from phospholipid to cholesterol, making cholesterol ester. The ester slips to the center, turning nascent HDL into a mature spherical particle

## Reverse transport

- Direct: HDL offloads cholesterol ester in the liver and recirculates; some HDL is endocytosed by the liver
- Indirect: cholesteryl ester transfer protein (CETP) swaps cholesterol ester for triglyceride on larger lipoproteins, and the liver takes up those ApoB particles

## Disorders

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Tangier disease | ABCA1 | Cholesterol and phospholipids aren't moved out of cells; low HDL; higher cardiovascular risk |
| LCAT deficiency | LCAT | Similar to Tangier disease; cholesterol deposits in the corneas and kidneys |

> Tangier disease (ABCA1) causes very low HDL because cells can't export cholesterol.

Library: Familial hyperlipoproteinemias
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Plasma Lipid Transport (Lipoproteins)

## Key findings

| Type | Defect | Elevated | Key findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| I, hyperchylomicronemia | LPL or ApoCII | Chylomicrons; high triglycerides, normal cholesterol | Pancreatitis, no atherosclerosis |
| IIa, hypercholesterolemia | LDL receptor | LDL; high cholesterol | Atherosclerosis |
| IIb, combined | VLDL overproduction | VLDL and IDL; high cholesterol and triglycerides | Atherosclerosis |
| III, dysbetalipoproteinemia | ApoE | Chylomicron remnants and IDL; moderate rise in cholesterol and triglycerides | Atherosclerosis |
| IV, hypertriglyceridemia | VLDL overproduction | VLDL; high triglycerides | Atherosclerosis |
| V, mixed | VLDL and chylomicrons | High triglycerides | Pancreatitis, no atherosclerosis |

## Why ApoE matters

As a chylomicron shrinks, it trades ApoCII for ApoE. Once small enough, it passes through the endothelium, and ApoE binds a receptor outside the vessel.

## Diagnosis

There's no natural cutoff between normal and abnormal lipid levels. A diagnosis needs a fasting lipid profile with abnormal values on two separate occasions.

> Type I (LPL or ApoCII) raises triglycerides and causes pancreatitis rather than atherosclerosis.

Library: Transamination and oxidative deamination
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Transamination

- Transaminases (aminotransferases) move an amino acid's amine group onto alpha-ketoglutarate, making glutamate; each is specific to its amino acid
- Alanine transaminase (ALT): alanine → pyruvate
- Aspartate aminotransferase (AST): aspartate → oxaloacetate
- Pyridoxal phosphate (PLP), the active form of vitamin B6, is the cofactor
- Glutamine synthetase: glutamate → glutamine

## Oxidative deamination

- Glutamate dehydrogenase in hepatocytes removes the amino group from glutamate as free ammonia for the urea cycle and regenerates alpha-ketoglutarate
- Glutaminase: glutamine → glutamate

## Urea cycle

The urea cycle has 5 steps, two of them in the mitochondria, and turns ammonia into urea.

> Transaminases need PLP, the active form of vitamin B6.

Library: Ammonia handling in brain, muscle, and kidney
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Key findings

| Tissue | How it handles ammonia |
|---|---|
| Brain | Glutamine synthetase captures ammonia as glutamine for safe transport; the liver breaks glutamine back to glutamate |
| Muscle | During exercise, ALT2 turns excess pyruvate into alanine instead of lactic acid; alanine goes to the liver for transamination and deamination |
| Kidney | Takes up glutamine and deaminates it with glutaminase and glutamate dehydrogenase; the ammonia neutralizes protons in tubule cells. Metabolic acidosis increases glutaminase |

## Liver failure

As liver function falls, ammonia builds up in the brain. At high ammonia levels, glutamate dehydrogenase can run in reverse and make glutamate from ammonia, depleting alpha-ketoglutarate and shutting down the citric acid cycle.

> High ammonia drains alpha-ketoglutarate and shuts down the citric acid cycle in the brain.

Library: Urea cycle disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Key findings

- All are recessive genetic defects in one of the urea cycle enzymes
- All cause hyperammonemia with low BUN, because urea isn't made

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Hyperammonemia type I | NAGS or CPS-I | High glutamine and alanine; no orotic aciduria. NAGS makes N-acetylglutamate, the allosteric activator of CPS-I, which runs the first step |
| Hyperammonemia type II | Ornithine transcarbamylase (OTC) | X-linked; can't form citrulline; orotic aciduria; high ammonia can cause coma |

- The allopurinol challenge test identifies carriers of OTC mutations

## Treatment

A low-protein diet to make less ammonia. Excess nitrogen can be captured with sodium benzoate, sodium phenylacetate, or phenylbutyrate.

> Orotic aciduria separates OTC deficiency from CPS-I deficiency.

Library: Liver and kidney disease: ALT, AST, BUN, and ammonia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Markers

- ALT and AST are found mainly in the liver, so high serum levels signal liver damage
- BUN is low in liver disease because urea isn't made

## Key findings

| Condition | BUN | Other findings |
|---|---|---|
| Liver disease and hepatic encephalopathy | Low | High ammonia, high AST and ALT; neuronal degeneration; sweet fecal smell |
| Chronic kidney disease | High | Urea can't be excreted; hyperammonemia; low GFR, fluid retention, high blood pressure |

- Both liver and kidney disease cause hyperammonemia by backing up ammonia excretion
- Lactulose binds ammonia and keeps it from diffusing back into circulation

> BUN is low in liver disease and high in kidney disease, but both cause hyperammonemia.

Library: Marasmus vs. kwashiorkor
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Key findings

| Condition | Diet | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Marasmus | Deficient in protein, carbohydrate, and fat | Like starvation; muscle breakdown and high gluconeogenesis |
| Kwashiorkor | Deficient in protein only | Low albumin, edema, fatty liver, high ALT and AST, low BUN, subcutaneous fat preserved |

> Kwashiorkor is protein-only deficiency, which causes low albumin and edema.

Library: H. pylori urease breath test
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Disorders of the Urea Cycle

## Key findings

- H. pylori has high urease activity
- A urease breath test shows high CO2 in the breath when urease activity is high
- The standard of care is triple therapy

> A positive urease breath test reflects H. pylori's high urease activity.

Library: Amino acid categories and plant diets
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

| Category | Definition |
|---|---|
| Glucogenic | Generates citric acid cycle intermediates or gluconeogenesis substrates |
| Ketogenic | Yields acetoacetate or its precursors |
| Conditional | Depends on intake of essential amino acids (tyrosine, made from phenylalanine) |

- Plant diets can be insufficient in lysine, methionine, phenylalanine, and tryptophan

> Tyrosine is conditional because it's made from phenylalanine.

Library: Branched-chain amino acid metabolism
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

- The branched-chain amino acids are leucine, isoleucine, and valine (LIV), and all are essential
- BCAA aminotransferase removes the amino group, needs vitamin B6, and produces the alpha-keto acids of the BCAAs
- Branched-chain alpha-ketoacid dehydrogenase (BCKD) has three subunits (E1, E2, E3) and breaks the keto acids down to acyl-CoA derivatives, which can become acetyl-CoA for the citric acid cycle

## Disorders by step

| Step | Disorder |
|---|---|
| BCKD | Maple syrup urine disease |
| After BCKD, leucine | Isovaleric acidemia |
| After BCKD, valine and isoleucine | Propionic and methylmalonic acidemia |

> BCKD is the enzyme lost in maple syrup urine disease.

Library: Maple syrup urine disease
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Cause

- Mutation in BCKD subunits E1, E2, or E3 (E3 is the most common and most serious), which stops BCAA catabolism and the citric acid cycle
- BCKD needs five vitamin-derived cofactors, and deficiencies of B1, B2, B3, or B5 can also cause it
- Alpha-keto acids of the BCAAs accumulate, and the buildup is believed to inhibit pyruvate and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, causing lactic acidosis

## Key findings

- Very high leucine
- Sweet or burnt-sugar odor in urine, sweat, and earwax
- Hypertonia or hypotonia
- Cyanotic episodes (blue skin, purple toes)
- Metabolic crisis with seizures, coma, and death if BCAA intake isn't restricted

## Treatment

Avoid foods containing all three branched-chain amino acids.

> Burnt-sugar odor with very high leucine points to maple syrup urine disease.

Library: Isovaleric, propionic, and methylmalonic acidemia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defective enzyme (cofactor) | Amino acids | Diet |
|---|---|---|---|
| Isovaleric acidemia | Isovaleryl-CoA dehydrogenase | Leucine | Avoid leucine |
| Propionic acidemia | Propionyl-CoA carboxylase (biotin) | Valine, isoleucine | Avoid valine and isoleucine |
| Methylmalonic acidemia | Methylmalonyl-CoA mutase (B12) | Valine, isoleucine | Avoid valine and isoleucine |

- All three occur after BCKD and look similar to MSUD
- Isovaleric acidemia gives body fluids a sweaty-feet odor
- Propionic acidemia shows high propionic acid; methylmalonic acidemia shows high methylmalonic acid
- Low biotin or B12 can cause similar findings; B12 deficiency also raises methylmalonic acid

> Sweaty-feet odor points to isovaleric acidemia, a defect in leucine breakdown.

Library: Pyruvate dehydrogenase deficiency
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

- Lactic acidemia can come from several defects, and most cause pyruvate to build up
- With defective pyruvate dehydrogenase, pyruvate can't become acetyl-CoA, so it's pushed to lactic acid by lactate dehydrogenase

## Treatment

A ketogenic diet, which doesn't feed into the blocked pathway.

> Pyruvate dehydrogenase deficiency is treated with a ketogenic diet.

Library: Phenylketonuria
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

Phenylalanine is converted to tyrosine, which depends on both an enzyme and a cofactor.

| Form | Defect | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Typical PKU | Phenylalanine hydroxylase | Restrict phenylalanine; supplement tyrosine |
| Atypical PKU | Dihydrobiopterin reductase, so tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4) isn't made | Restrict phenylalanine; supplement tyrosine and BH4 |

- Atypical PKU doesn't respond to tyrosine supplementation alone, which helps tell the two apart
- Excess phenylalanine becomes phenylpyruvate (a phenyl ketone), causing a musty or mousy odor
- Diagnosis: high phenylalanine with a musty or mousy odor
- Other findings: failure to thrive, CNS symptoms, hypopigmentation (fair hair, blue eyes, pale skin)

## Maternal PKU

A mother with PKU can cause defects in her baby, so she needs a phenylalanine-restricted diet with tyrosine supplementation during pregnancy.

> Musty odor with hypopigmentation points to PKU.

Library: Alkaptonuria, albinism, and tyrosinemia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defective enzyme | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Alkaptonuria | Homogentisic acid oxidase | Homogentisic acid builds up; black urine, sweat, and earwax; dark spots in the eyes; joint pain and reduced range of motion; appears in adulthood |
| Albinism | Tyrosinase | Melanin can't be made from tyrosine; white hair, pale skin, low iris pigment |
| Tyrosinemia type I | Fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase | High tyrosine, fumarylacetoacetate, and succinylacetone; liver and kidney dysfunction, jaundice, cabbage-like odor |
| Tyrosinemia type II | Tyrosine aminotransferase | Not detailed |

## Treatment of tyrosinemia type I

A phenylalanine- and tyrosine-restricted diet, plus nitisinone to prevent homogentisic acid formation.

> Cabbage-like odor with liver and kidney dysfunction points to tyrosinemia type I.

Library: Catecholamine and serotonin disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

- Tyrosine makes the catecholamines dopamine, norepinephrine, and epinephrine
- Monoamine oxidase (MAO) breaks down all three
- Tryptophan makes serotonin, a monoamine neurotransmitter

## Related disorders

| Disorder | Key findings |
|---|---|
| Parkinson disease | Low dopamine; TRAP: tremor, rigidity, akinesia, postural instability |
| Neuroblastoma | HVA and VMA in urine |
| Pheochromocytoma | High VMA and metanephrine; 5 P's: pressure (headache), pain, pallor, palpitations, perspiration |
| Paraganglioma | Catecholamine-producing carotid body tumor |
| Dopamine beta-hydroxylase deficiency | High serum dopamine |

> Pheochromocytoma raises urinary VMA and metanephrine.

Library: Homocystinuria
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 3: Metabolic Foundations / Clinical Aspects of Amino Acid Metabolism

## Key findings

- Defective MS, MTHFR, or CBS (cystathionine beta-synthase) in the methionine/folate cycle
- High homocysteine in blood and urine
- Thrombosis, stroke, heart attack, downward dislocation of the lens, and scoliosis
- Autosomal recessive

## Look-alike

| Condition | Body type | Lens | Inheritance |
|---|---|---|---|
| Homocystinuria | Tall, slender, long limbs and fingers | Dislocates down | Autosomal recessive |
| Marfan syndrome | Tall, slender, long limbs and fingers | Dislocates up | Autosomal dominant |

> Homocystinuria dislocates the lens downward; Marfan syndrome dislocates it upward.

Library: DNA hybridization and repeat sequences
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Key terms

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Denaturation | Separation of the two strands, with loss of helical structure |
| Renaturation (annealing) | Complementary strands pair back into a double helix |
| Hybridization | Lab technique based on complementary strands pairing; low-stringency conditions let closely related sequences hybridize, so it can't tell apart disease-causing point mutations |
| 3' overhang | The 3' end of eukaryotic DNA is longer than the 5' end |

## Repeat sequences

| Type | Repeat length | Significance |
|---|---|---|
| Minisatellites | 1-100 bp | Repeat number varies between people (VNTR polymorphism); used in paternity testing and criminal investigations |
| Microsatellites | 2-6 bp | Hotspots for replication errors; involved in disease-causing mutations |

> VNTRs (minisatellites) are used for paternity testing; microsatellites are hotspots for replication errors.

Library: DNA polymerases, topoisomerases, and telomerase
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Polymerases

- DNA-dependent DNA polymerases synthesize DNA from a template in the 5' → 3' direction and have 3' → 5' exonuclease activity for proofreading
- Pol gamma is the mitochondrial polymerase
- Telomerase is a reverse transcriptase (an RNA-dependent DNA polymerase)

## Topoisomerases

| Type | Cut | Supercoils relaxed | ATP |
|---|---|---|---|
| Type I | Single-strand nick | One | Not needed |
| Type II | Double-strand break | Two | Needed |

## Drugs that target topoisomerases

| Drug | Target | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Irinotecan, topotecan, camptothecin | Topoisomerase I | Colon and ovarian cancer (irinotecan) |
| Etoposide, teniposide | Topoisomerase II | Testicular cancer, leukemia, lymphoma |
| Doxorubicin, daunorubicin | Topoisomerase II (also intercalate and make free radicals) | Solid tumors, leukemias, lymphomas |
| Fluoroquinolones (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin) | DNA gyrase (bacterial topoisomerase II) | Mainly gram-negative infections |

> Type I topoisomerases cut one strand without ATP; type II cut both strands and need ATP.

Library: Sources of DNA damage and the Ames test
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Endogenous sources

- Microsatellites are hotspots for mismatches; stable loops can form during replication and change the repeat number with each cycle
- Spontaneous deamination or loss of bases
- Deamination of cytosine makes uracil, which is recognized as foreign and excised
- Deamination of methylated cytosine makes thymine, which isn't recognized as foreign, so the code changes
- Insertion of transposons

## Exogenous sources

| Source | Damage |
|---|---|
| Ionizing radiation (X-rays, gamma rays, cosmic rays) | ROS cause single- and double-strand breaks and base loss |
| UV radiation | Dimerization of adjacent pyrimidines, especially thymine |
| Chemicals | Alkylating agents, crosslinking agents, base analogs, intercalators, chemotherapy |

- Mutations can occur anywhere: coding regions, regulatory regions, or introns

## Ames test

Salmonella is modified so it can't grow without histidine. A mutagen causes mutations that restore its ability to make histidine, so more colonies grow.

> Deaminated methylcytosine becomes thymine, which repair enzymes don't recognize as foreign.

Library: Trinucleotide repeat disorders and anticipation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Anticipation

With each generation, symptoms appear at an earlier age. It usually results from trinucleotide repeats that expand during replication.

## Key findings

| Disorder | Repeat, gene | Inheritance | Key findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fragile X syndrome | CGG, FMR1 | X-linked dominant | Enlarged jaw, ears, and testes |
| Friedreich ataxia | GAA, frataxin (chromosome 9) | Autosomal recessive | Cerebellar and spinal cord degeneration, kyphoscoliosis, pes cavus; hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is a common cause of death |
| Huntington disease | CAG, huntingtin (chromosome 4) | Autosomal dominant | Atrophy of the caudate and putamen; chorea progressing to depression, dementia, aggression; onset between 20 and 50 |
| Myotonic dystrophy | CTG, DMPK | Autosomal dominant | Cataracts, toupee (early balding), gonadal atrophy, myotonia, muscle wasting, arrhythmias |

> Anticipation (earlier onset each generation) points to a trinucleotide repeat disorder.

Library: DNA repair pathways
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Key findings

| Pathway | What it fixes | How |
|---|---|---|
| Direct reversal | O6-alkylguanine, the main lesion from alkylating agents | Methylguanine methyltransferase (MGMT) removes the adduct |
| Mismatch repair (MMR) | Replication errors, small insertions and deletions | Mut proteins recognize the mismatch and nick the new strand |
| Base excision repair (BER) | Damaged bases (such as oxidative damage) and single-strand breaks | Glycosylases remove the base; polymerase and ligase fill the gap |
| Nucleotide excision repair (NER) | Helix-distorting lesions: thymine dimers, bulky adducts, backbone changes | An exonuclease and helicase remove a short segment; polymerase fills it; the global NER pathway scans the whole genome |
| Homologous recombination (HR) | Double-strand breaks | Uses the duplicate copy as a template |
| Non-homologous end joining (NHEJ) | Double-strand breaks | Joins ends directly with no template; much more error prone |

- Overexpression of MGMT can make tumors resistant to alkylating drugs
- In prokaryotes, mismatch repair tells the parent strand from the daughter strand by DNA methylation
- Double-strand break repair is the only kind that can't use the complementary strand as a template

> UV thymine dimers are repaired by nucleotide excision repair.

Library: DNA repair disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Replication and Repair

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
